intervals=None, \\n    trials_df=None, \\n    allow_nans=False, \\n    n_workers=os.cpu_count(), \\n    **kwargs\\n):\\n    \"\"\"\\n    Format a single session-wide array of target data into a list of interval-based arrays.\\n\\n    Note: the bin size of the returned data will only be equal to the input `binsize` if that value\\n    evenly divides `align_interval`; for example if `align_interval=(0, 0.2)` and `binsize=0.10`,\\n    then the returned data will have the correct binsize. If `align_interval=(0, 0.2)` and\\n    `binsize=0.06` then the returned data will not have the correct binsize.\\n\\n    Parameters\\n    ----------\\n    target_times : array-like\\n        time in seconds for each sample\\n    target_vals : array-like\\n        data samples\\n    intervals : \\n        array of time intervals for each recording chunk including trials and non-trials\\n    trials_df : pd.DataFrame\\n        requires a column that matches `align_event`\\n    align_event : str\\n        event to align interval to\\n        firstMovement_times | stimOn_times | feedback_times\\n    align_interval : tuple\\n        (align_begin, align_end); time in seconds relative to align_event\\n    binsize : float\\n        size of individual bins in interval\\n    allow_nans : bool, optional\\n        False to skip intervals with >0 NaN values in target data\\n\\n    Returns\\n    -------\\n    tuple\\n        - (list): time in seconds for each interval\\n        - (list): data for each interval\\n        - (array-like): mask of good intervals (True) and bad intervals (False)\\n\\n    \"\"\"\\n\\n    binsize = kwargs[\\'binsize\\']\\n    align_interval = kwargs[\\'time_window\\']\\n    interval_len = align_interval[1] - align_interval[0]\\n\\n    if trials_df is not None:\\n        align_event = kwargs[\\'align_time\\']\\n        align_times = trials_df[align_event].values\\n        interval_begs = align_times + align_interval[0]\\n        interval_ends = align_times + align_interval[1]\\n    else:\\n        assert intervals is not None, \\'Require intervals to segment the recording into chunks including trials and non-trials.\\'\\n        interval_begs, interval_ends = intervals.T\\n\\n    n_intervals = len(interval_begs)\\n\\n    if np.all(np.isnan(interval_begs)) or np.all(np.isnan(interval_ends)):\\n        print(\\'interval times all nan\\')\\n        good_interval = np.nan * np.ones(interval_begs.shape[0])\\n        target_times_list = []\\n        target_vals_list = []\\n        return target_times_list, target_vals_list, good_interval\\n\\n    # np.ceil because we want to make sure our bins contain all data\\n    n_bins = int(np.ceil(interval_len / binsize))\\n\\n    # split data into intervals\\n    idxs_beg = np.searchsorted(target_times, interval_begs, side=\\'right\\')\\n    idxs_end = np.searchsorted(target_times, interval_ends, side=\\'left\\')\\n    target_times_og_list = [target_times[ib:ie] for ib, ie in zip(idxs_beg, idxs_end)]\\n    target_vals_og_list = [target_vals[ib:ie] for ib, ie in zip(idxs_beg, idxs_end)]\\n\\n    # interpolate and store\\n    target_times_list = [None for _ in range(len(target_times_og_list))]\\n    target_vals_list = [None for _ in range(len(target_times_og_list))]\\n    good_interval = [None for _ in range(len(target_times_og_list))]\\n    skip_reasons = [None for _ in range(len(target_times_og_list))]\\n\\n    @globalize\\n    def interpolate_behavior(target):\\n        # We use interval_idx to track the interval order while working with p.imap_unordered()\\n        interval_idx, target_time, target_vals = target\\n\\n        is_good_interval, x_interp, y_interp = False, None, None\\n        \\n        if len(target_vals) == 0:\\n            skip_reason = \\'target data not present\\'\\n            return interval_idx, is_good_interval, x_interp, y_interp, skip_reason\\n        if np.sum(np.isnan(target_vals)) > 0 and not allow_nans:\\n            skip_reason = \\'nans in target data\\'\\n            return interval_idx, is_good_interval, x_interp, y_interp, skip_reason\\n        if np.isnan(interval_begs[interval_idx]) or np.isnan(interval_ends[interval_idx]):\\n            skip_reason = \\'bad interval data\\'\\n            return interval_idx, is_good_interval, x_interp, y_interp, skip_reason\\n        if np.abs(interval_begs[interval_idx] - target_time[0]) > binsize:\\n            skip_reason = \\'target data starts too late\\'\\n            return interval_idx, is_good_interval, x_interp, y_interp, skip_reason\\n        if np.abs(interval_ends[interval_idx] - target_time[-1]) > binsize:\\n            skip_reason = \\'target data ends too early\\'\\n            return interval_idx, is_good_interval, x_interp, y_interp, skip_reason\\n\\n        is_good_interval, skip_reason = True, None\\n        x_interp = np.linspace(interval_begs[interval_idx] + binsize, interval_ends[interval_idx], n_bins)\\n        if len(target_vals.shape) > 1 and target_vals.shape[1] > 1:\\n            n_dims = target_vals.shape[1]\\n            y_interp_tmps = []\\n            for n in range(n_dims):\\n                y_interp_tmps.append(interp1d(\\n                    target_time, target_vals[:, n], kind=\\'linear\\',\\n                    fill_value=\\'extrapolate\\')(x_interp))\\n            y_interp = np.hstack([y[:, None] for y in y_interp_tmps])\\n        else:\\n            y_interp = interp1d(\\n                target_time, target_vals, kind=\\'linear\\', fill_value=\\'extrapolate\\')(x_interp)\\n        return interval_idx, is_good_interval, x_interp, y_interp, skip_reason\\n\\n    with multiprocessing.Pool(processes=n_workers) as p:\\n        targets = list(zip(np.arange(n_intervals), target_times_og_list, target_vals_og_list))\\n        with tqdm(total=n_intervals) as pbar:\\n            for res in p.imap_unordered(interpolate_behavior, targets):\\n                pbar.update()\\n                good_interval[res[0]] = res[1]\\n                target_times_list[res[0]] = res[2]\\n                target_vals_list[res[0]] = res[3]\\n                skip_reasons[res[0]] = res[-1]\\n        pbar.close()\\n        p.close()\\n\\n    return target_times_list, target_vals_list, np.array(good_interval), skip_reasons    \\n\\n\\ndef load_anytime_behaviors(one, eid, n_workers=os.cpu_count()):\\n\\n    behaviors = [\\n Unlike these behavior-only approaches, we also \\nincorporate neural data. Other studies have applied HMMs to \\nexplain neural activity directly, 54–58 whereas we infer states \\nthat generate decoder estimates combining neural and behav-\\nioral information. Related work such as BehaveNet 59 integrates \\nbehavioral video data with autoregressive HMMs to smooth pre-\\ndictions. Our focus is on exploiting trial-to-trial correlations in IBL \\ndata, but similar history dependence is common across neuro-\\nscience experiments. 16,60,61 Incorporating task and behavioral \\nhistory is therefore valuable not only for improving decoding \\nbut also for understanding learning and memory mechanisms. \\nTechnological advances now allow for the simultaneous \\ncollection of multiple data modalities, such as local field poten-\\ntials and calcium imaging, in neuroscience experiments. RRRs \\nextend beyond neural decoding to tasks like neural encod-\\ning 21,38,62 and inter-region activity prediction. 63 Future directions \\ninclude incorporating additional data modalities, expanding the \\nmodel to new tasks, and relaxing the assumption of shared tem-\\nporal bases across sessions, which may not hold when animals \\nare at different learning stages or performance levels. For multi-\\nsession state-space models, nonlinear time series and dynam-\\nical systems 64–66 could better capture complex latent behavioral \\ndynamics. Finally, all models are compatible with density-based \\ndecoding, 67 enabling decoding from unsorted spike features, \\nwhich may further improve accuracy.\\nRESOURCE AVAILABILITY\\nLead contact\\nRequests for further information and resources should be directed to and will \\nbe fulfilled by the lead contact, Yizi Zhang (yz4123@columbia.edu).\\nMaterials availability\\nThis study did not generate new reagents.\\nData and code availability\\n• Code to download the neural and behavioral data for reproducing this \\nstudy is publicly available at https://docs.internationalbrainlab.org/ \\nindex.html as of the publication date.\\n• All original code is publicly available at https://github.com/yzhang511/ \\nneural_decoding with the DOI https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.1731 \\n7498.\\n• Additional information for analysis is available from the lead contact \\nupon request.\\nACKNOWLEDGMENTS\\nThis work was funded by grants from the Wellcome Trust (209558 and 216324), \\nthe National Institutes of Health (1U19NS123716 and K99MH128772), the\\nSimons Foundation, and the National Science Foundation and DoD OUSD \\n(R&E) under Cooperative Agreement PHY-2229929 (The NSF AI Institute for \\nArtificial and Natural Intelligence). For prior, we also \\nalign trials to the stimulus onset, including neural activity from 0.6 s to 0.1 s pre-onset. The prior represents the mice’s estimate of the \\nstimulus side probability. We use the same decoding window as in the previous study, 27 focusing on the period with minimal wheel \\nmovements. Within each trial, we segment neural activity into 50-ms non-overlapping time bins. For each time bin, we bin spike \\ncounts using all neurons, sorted by Kilosort 2.5, 77 from each session. For dynamic behaviors (wheel speed and whisker motion en-\\nergy), we select the first movement onset as the alignment event, and decode the behavior starting at the alignment event and ending \\nat 1 s after the alignment event. The neural activity within each trial is binned into non-overlapping 20 ms bins. For each time bin, we \\nsimilarly bin spike counts using all neurons from each session.\\nIBL trial-unaligned dataset\\nWe focus only on wheel speed and whisker motion energy, as variables like choice and prior are only defined during experimental \\ntrials, which are periods when stimuli are presented and mice respond for rewards. Unlike the trial-aligned dataset, we do not align \\nneural activity and behavior to a specific event onset. Instead, we segment the continuous Neuropixels recordings into 2-s snippets \\nto construct the trial-unaligned dataset for each session. These snippets cover intervals both during and outside experimental trials, \\ncapturing the repetitive, structured behaviors within trials as well as the more spontaneous behaviors outside of trials.\\nll\\nOPEN ACCESS \\n Article\\ne8 Neuron 114, 1–16.e1–e11, February 4, 2026\\nPlease cite this article in press as: Zhang et al., Exploiting correlations across trials and behavioral sessions to improve neural decoding, Neuron (2025), \\nhttps://doi.org/10.1016/j.neuron.2025.10.026\\n\\n===== PAGE 26 =====\\nAllen dataset\\nTo use reduced-rank regression models, we decode 3 behavior variables: Gabors, static gratings, and running speed. The Gabors \\nvariable has 3 discrete orientation classes, while the static gratings variable has 6. Running speed, in contrast, is a continuous trial-\\nunaligned variable recorded during periods of spontaneous behavior. For Gabors and static gratings, we align trials to the respective \\nstimulus onset and extract neural activity within a 200 ms window around the onset. For running speed, we divide the continuous \\nrecording into 1-s snippets. For all variables, we use a time bin size of 10 ms to bin neural activity.\\nTo apply BMM-HMM, we also decode the licks. In the Allen datasets, mice view a continuous stream of briefly presented visual \\nstimuli and receive water rewards for correctly detecting changes in stimulus identity. To relate neural activity to behavior, the timing \\nof behavioral responses, licks, is also recorded. Each Neuropixels recording session is divided into five stimulus blocks, but licking \\noccurs exclusively during the ‘‘active’’ period, which lasts approximately 2000 to 3000 s. During this period, each visual stimulus is \\nupdated roughly every 0.2 s. We segment the active period into fixed 0.2-s intervals and labeled each interval as 1 if at least one lick \\noccurred, and 0 otherwise. For each interval, we use a time bin size of 10 ms. For each session, we decode licks using neural activity \\nfrom the recorded subset of regions of interest, DG, CA1, CA3, VISp, VISa, LP, and TH (the medial division of the posterior parahip-\\npocampal cortex). For example, if a session records from DG, CA1, and LP, all three are used in the decoder. Regions outside this set \\nare excluded from the analysis, even if recorded. Region naming follows the IBL brain atlas conventions.\\nPrimate random target dataset\\nThis dataset consists of 15 min of continuous reaching behavior, manually segmented into 1351 600 ms ‘‘trials.’’ It includes record-\\nings from 130 neurons along with simultaneously tracked hand kinematics. We use the preprocessed version of the dataset provided \\nby Pei et al., 20 and apply a 20 ms bin size to discretize neural activity and behavior into 30 time bins per trial. Our decoding target is \\nfinger velocity, represented by velocity components along both the X and Y axes.\\nHyperparameter selection\\nFor static variables (choice, prior, Gabors, static gratings), the target behavior in each trial is decoded using the full neural activity from \\nthat trial. For dynamic behaviors (wheel speed, whisker motion energy, running speed, finger velocity), the target behavior at each \\ntime bin t is decoded using the entire neural activity across all time bins within the corresponding trial. For multi-session models \\napplied to the IBL dataset, we use both multi-session and multi-region reduced-rank regression models to decode static and dy-\\nnamic behaviors. In contrast, for the Allen and primate random target datasets, we do not include multi-region reduced-rank regres-\\nsion models, as our objective is not to perform region-specific decoding for these datasets. For discrete behavior variables (e.g., \\nchoice and licks), we employ both single- and multi-session BMM-HMM models. To decode prior, we use both single- and multi-ses-\\nsion LG-AR1 models. Decoder performance is evaluated using accuracy and AUC for discrete variables, Pearson correlation for \\nprior, and R 2 for dynamic behaviors.\\nFor our linear baselines, we use L2-regularized logistic regression for discrete-valued variables and ridge regression for prior and\\ndynamic behavioral variables. Regularization strength is selected via scikit-learn’s GridSearchCV function, from the set \\n{ \\n10 k ⃒ ⃒ k = \\x00\\n4; \\x00 3;…;4 \\n} \\n. Both reduced-rank regression models and MLPs are trained using gradient descent in PyTorch, with the AdamW opti-\\nmizer and a cosine annealing learning rate scheduler. All models are trained for 2000 epochs with a batch size of 16, and the best \\nmodel, determined by the lowest validation loss, is selected for test set decoding. For single-session MLP and reduced-rank regres-\\nsion models, hyperparameter search is performed over the value ranges in Table S2, using Ray Tune in Python. We randomly sample \\n30 models from the specified range for this search. For multi-session and multi-region reduced-rank regression models, extensive \\nhyperparameter tuning is not required as for the single-session models, since the model is less prone to overfitting. As a result, we \\nonly perform a search over the rank of the reduced-rank regression models, while fixing the learning rate at 0.01 and weight decay at \\n1, based on results from pilot studies.\\nsolutions and hold them fixed while inferring the latent z k and improved decoder outputs ~d k , as if we know the true data-generating\\nmechanism. \\'nneurons\\': nneurons,\\n        }\\n\\n\\ndef train_decoder(neural: list, input: list, output: list, metadata: dict = {}, **kwargs):\\n    \"\"\"\\n    Trains a common decoder for all sessions to predict output from neural and input data.\\n    Jointly learns projection matrices for each session and separate shared decoders for each output dimension.\\n\\n    Inputs:\\n        neural: neural activity. neural is a list of length nsessions, neural[session] is a list\\n                of length ntrials[session], and neural[session][trial] is a numpy array of shape (nneurons[session], T[session][trial]),\\n                dtype = float32, giving the neural activity for that trial\\n        input: variables beyond neural activity the decoder should input, e.g. the stimulis, condition. input is a list of\\n                length nsessions, input[session] is a list of length ntrials[session], and input[session][trial] is a numpy array of\\n                shape (dinput, T[session][trial]), dtype = float32. with the input variable(s) for each timepoint.\\n                dinput is the number of inputs. For discrete inputs, use a one-hot encoding.\\n        output: variables we want to decode. output is a list of length nsessions, output[session] is a list of length ntrials[session],\\n                and output[session][trial] is a numpy array of shape (doutput, T[session][trial]) of dtype = int with\\n                categorical values for each timepoint. Each output[idx, t] is an integer from 0 to ncategories[idx]-1.\\n        metadata: optional dict with additional information about the experiment.\\n\\n        Algorithm hyperparameters can be passed as keyword arguments:\\n        npcs: number of projected dimensions to use (default: 10)\\n        num_epochs: number of training epochs (default: 200)\\n        lr: learning rate (default: 0.001)\\n        l1_weight: L1 regularization weight for projection matrices (default: 1e-4)\\n        svd_max_samples: maximum number of timepoints to use for SVD initialization (default: 50000).\\n                         If a session has more timepoints than this, a random subset will be used.\\n        svd_max_neurons: maximum number of neurons to use for SVD initialization (default: 2000).\\n                         If a session has more neurons than this, random Gaussian projection will be used.\\n                         The final weight is the product of the random projection and SVD matrices.\\n        ncategories: optional list of length doutput giving number of categories for each output dimension.\\n                     If not provided, will be inferred from data.\\n        balanced_loss: if True, use class-weighted cross-entropy loss to compensate for class imbalance.\\n                       Weights are inverse of class frequency. Default: False.\\n\\n    Returns:\\n        model: dict with trained decoder parameters. dict with the keys:\\n            \\'projection\\': list of length nsessions, projection[session] is a torch tensor of shape (npcs, nneurons[session])\\n            \\'decoder\\': list of length doutput, decoder[out_dim] is a torch linear layer for that output dimension\\n            \\'ncategories\\': list of length doutput, ncategories[out_dim] is the number of categories for that output\\n            \\'device\\': torch device used for training\\n\\n    \"\"\"\\n\\n    model = {\\n        \\'projection\\': [],\\n        \\'decoder\\': [],\\n        \\'ncategories\\': [],\\n        \\'device\\': None\\n    }\\n\\n    npcs = kwargs.get(\\'npcs\\', 10)\\n    num_epochs = kwargs.get(\\'num_epochs\\', 200)\\n    lr = kwargs.get(\\'lr\\', 0.001)\\n    l1_weight = kwargs.get(\\'l1_weight\\', 1e-4)\\n    device_str = kwargs.get(\\'device\\', None)\\n    balanced_loss = kwargs.get(\\'balanced_loss\\', False)\\n\\n    nsessions = len(neural)\\n    if device_str is not None:\\n        device = torch.device(device_str)\\n    else:\\n        device = torch.device(\\'cuda\\' if torch.cuda.is_available() else \\'cpu\\')\\n\\n    model[\\'device\\'] = device\\n    print(\\'Using device:\\', device)\\n\\n    # Prepare training data using helper function\\n    session_data, dinput, doutput = _prepare_session_data(neural, input, output, device)\\n\\n    # Determine number of categories for each output dimension\\n    if \\'ncategories\\' in kwargs:\\n        ncategories = kwargs[\\'ncategories\\']\\n    else:\\n        # Session is the OUTER loop so each one is fetched once for all output\\n        # dimensions. With session inner, session_data[session] would rebuild the\\n        # same session\\'s tensors doutput times. The result does not depend on the\\n        # order.\\n        max_cat = [0] * doutput\\n        for session in range(nsessions):\\n            batch = session_data[session]\\n            if batch is None:\\n                continue\\n            for out_dim in range(doutput):\\n                max_cat[out_dim] = max(max_cat[out_dim],\\n                                       int(batch[\\'output\\'][:, out_dim].max().item()))\\n        ncategories = [m + 1 for m in max_cat]\\n\\n    model[\\'ncategories\\'] = ncategories\\n\\n    # Compute class weights (always, for use in chance computation and optionally for balanced loss)\\n    class_weights = []\\n    class_fractions = []  # Store fractions for chance computation\\n    # Session outer, output dimension inner, for the same reason as ncategories\\n    # above: one fetch per session instead of doutput of them. Summation order\\n    # within a class count is unchanged.\\n    class_counts_all = [np.zeros(ncategories[out_dim]) for out_dim in range(doutput)]\\n    for session in range(nsessions):\\n        batch = session_data[session]\\n        if batch is None:\\n            continue\\n        for out_dim in range(doutput):\\n            outputs = batch[\\'output\\'][:, out_dim].cpu().numpy()\\n            for c in range(ncategories[out_dim]):\\n                class_counts_all[out_dim][c] += np.sum(outputs == c)\\n\\n    for out_dim in range(doutput):\\n        class_counts = class_counts_all[out_dim]\\n        # Store class fractions for chance computation\\n        total = np.sum(class_counts)\\n        fractions = class_counts / total\\n        class_fractions.append(fractions)\\n        # Compute weights as inverse of frequency (with smoothing to avoid division by zero)\\n        weights = total / (ncategories[out_dim] * np.maximum(class_counts, 1))\\n        # Normalize weights to sum to 1 (so each output dimension is equally weighted)\\n        weights = weights / np.sum(weights)\\n        class_weights.append(torch.tensor(weights, dtype=torch.float32, device=device))\\n\\n    model[\\'class_weights\\'] = class_weights\\n    model[\\'class_fractions\\'] = class_fractions\\n\\n    if balanced_loss:\\n        print(f\"Using balanced loss with class weights:\")\\n        for out_dim in range(doutput):\\n            print(f\"  Output {out_dim}: {class_weights[out_dim].cpu().numpy()}\")\\n\\n    # Initialize projection matrices for each session\\n    svd_max_samples = kwargs.get(\\'svd_max_samples\\', 50000)  # Max timepoints to use for SVD initialization\\n    svd_max_neurons = kwargs.get(\\'svd_max_neurons\\', 2000)   # Max neurons to use for SVD initialization\\n    projection_layers = []\\n    for session in range(nsessions):\\n        # Bound once: session_data converts on every subscript, so the four reads\\n        # below would otherwise rebuild this session\\'s tensors four times.\\n        batch = session_data[session]\\n        if batch is None:\\n            # A session with no trials has no neuron count to size a layer with. Held as\\n            # None so that projection_layers stays indexed by session; every read of it is\\n            # guarded by the same emptiness check that skips the session\\'s data.\\n            projection_layers.append(None)\\n            continue\\n        nneurons = batch[\\'nneurons\\']\\n        proj = nn.Linear(nneurons, npcs, bias=False).to(device)\\n\\n        # Initialize with SVD for stability (use V from SVD of neural data)\\n        # For neural data with shape (n_timepoints, n_neurons), SVD gives principal components in V\\n        # Use random subsets if data is too large\\n        n_timepoints = batch[\\'neural\\'].shape[0]\\n\\n        # Sample timepoints if needed\\n        if n_timepoints > svd_max_samples:\\n            time_indices = torch.randperm(n_timepoints)[:svd_max_samples]\\n            neural_subset = batch[\\'neural\\'][time_indices, :]\\n            print(f\"Session {session}: Using {svd_max_samples} of {n_timepoints} timepoints for SVD initialization\")\\n        else:\\n            neural_subset = batch[\\'neural\\']\\n\\n        # Use random projection if too many neurons\\n        if nneurons > svd_max_neurons:\\n            # Create random Gaussian projection matrix: (n_neurons, svd_max_neurons)\\n            P = torch.randn(nneurons, svd_max_neurons)\\n            # Project neural data: (n_timepoints, n_neurons) @ (n_neurons, svd_max_neurons) = (n_timepoints, svd_max_neurons)\\n            neural_projected = neural_subset @ P\\n            print(f\"Session {session}: Using random projection to {svd_max_neurons} of {nneurons} neurons for SVD initialization\")\\n\\n            # SVD on projected data\\n            _, _, V = torch.svd(neural_projected, some=True)\\n            # V has shape (svd_max_neurons, min(n_timepoints_subset, svd_max_neurons))\\n\\n            # Compose: final weight = (P @ V[:, :npcs]).T = V[:, :npcs].T @ P.T\\n            n_components = min(npcs, V.shape[1])\\n            proj.weight.data[:n_components, :] = (V[:, :n_components].T @ P.T).to(device).clone()\\n        else:\\n            # Standard SVD without random projection\\n            _, _, V = torch.svd(neural_subset, some=True)\\n            # V has shape (n_neurons, min(n_timepoints_subset, n_neurons))\\n            n_components = min(npcs, V.shape[1])\\n            proj.weight.data[:n_components, :] = V[:, :n_components].T.to(device).clone()\\n\\n        # Initialize remaining components randomly if needed\\n        if n_components < npcs:\\n            nn.init.orthogonal_(proj.weight.data[n_components:, :])\\n\\n        projection_layers.append(proj)\\n\\n    # Initialize separate decoders for each output dimension\\n    decoders = []\\n    for out_dim in range(doutput):\\n        decoder = nn.Linear(npcs + dinput, ncategories[out_dim]).to(device)\\n        decoders.append(decoder)\\n\\n    # Collect all parameters\\n    all_params = []\\n    for proj in projection_layers:\\n        if proj is None:          # session with no trials, nothing to train\\n            continue\\n        all_params.extend(proj.parameters())\\n    for decoder in decoders:\\n        all_params.extend(decoder.parameters())\\n\\n    optimizer = optim.Adam(all_params, lr=lr)\\n\\n    # Loss function with reduction=\\'none\\' for manual weighting (matches torch_brain)\\n    criterion = nn.CrossEntropyLoss(reduction=\\'none\\')\\n\\n    # Training loop\\n    # Data stays on CPU; each session is moved to GPU one at a time.\\n    # backward() is called per session to free the computation graph before\\n    # loading the next session. Gradients accumulate across sessions.\\n    for epoch in range(num_epochs):\\n        epoch_loss = 0.\\n        optimizer.zero_grad()\\n\\n        # iter_sessions rather than session_data[session]: every session is\\n        # converted once per epoch, and converting the next few on other threads\\n        # overlaps that with this session\\'s forward and backward pass.\\n        for session, batch in session_data.iter_sessions():\\n            if batch is None:\\n                continue\\n            # Move this session\\'s data to device\\n            neural_batch = batch[\\'neural\\'].to(device)\\n            input_batch = batch[\\'input\\'].to(device)\\n            output_batch = batch[\\'output\\'].to(device)\\n\\n            # Forward pass - shared projection\\n            projected = projection_layers[session](neural_batch)\\n            combined = torch.cat([projected, input_batch], dim=1)\\n\\n            # Compute loss for each output dimension\\n            session_loss = torch.tensor(0., device=device)\\n            for out_dim in range(doutput):\\n                logits = decoders[out_dim](combined)\\n                target = output_batch[:, out_dim]\\n                loss_per_sample = criterion(logits, target)\\n\\n                if balanced_loss:\\n                    # Manual per-sample weighting (matches torch_brain exactly)\\n                    sample_weights = class_weights[out_dim][target]\\n                    session_loss += (sample_weights * loss_per_sample).sum() / sample_weights.sum()\\n                else:\\n                    session_loss += loss_per_sample.mean()\\n\\n            # Add L1 regularization on current session\\'s projection matrix\\n            if l1_weight > 0:\\n                l1_loss = torch.sum(torch.abs(projection_layers[session].weight))\\n                session_loss += l1_weight * l1_loss\\n\\n            # Backward per session: frees computation graph, accumulates gradients\\n            session_loss.backward()\\n            epoch_loss += session_loss.item()\\n\\n        optimizer.step()\\n\\n        if ((epoch + 1) % 10 == 0) or (epoch < 10):\\n            # Loss is sum of per-output-dim means across sessions\\n            # Normalize by (nsessions * doutput) to get average per output per session\\n            train_loss_normalized = epoch_loss / (nsessions * doutput)\\n            print(f\\'Epoch [{epoch+1}/{num_epochs}], Loss: {train_loss_normalized:.6f}\\')\\n\\n    # Store trained parameters. A session with no trials contributes None, keeping\\n    # model[\\'projection\\'] indexed by session for predict(), which reads it only for\\n    # sessions that have trials.\\n    for session in range(nsessions):\\n        proj = projection_layers[session]\\n        model[\\'projection\\'].append(None if proj is None else proj.weight.data.clone())\\n    model[\\'decoder\\'] = decoders\\n\\n    return model\\n\\ndef predict(neural: list, input: list, model: dict, sessionid: list | None = None, metadata: dict = {}):\\n    \"\"\"\\n'}]\n\nSTEP 60\nOBS: [{'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'Script
